\title{Uniformization of complete K\"ahler manifolds\\[2pt]
       with positive bisectional curvature}
\author{OpenAI}
\date{September 23, 2026}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
Every complete, connected, noncompact K\"ahler manifold with strictly
positive holomorphic bisectional curvature is biholomorphic to complex
Euclidean space. This settles Yau's uniformization conjecture in every
complex dimension, with no additional hypotheses on volume growth,
curvature bounds, or topology.
\end{abstract}

\section*{What this paper proves, in plain words}
A K\"ahler manifold is a space that looks locally like $\C^n$ and carries
a metric whose imaginary part is a closed two-form. Positive holomorphic
bisectional curvature says the space bends like a round sphere in every
complex two-plane. The theorem says: if such a space has no boundary and
no holes in the metric sense (it is complete and noncompact), then as a
complex manifold it is exactly $\C^n$.

The proof runs K\"ahler--Ricci flow $h_t$ (an equation that shrinks the
metric in the direction of its Ricci curvature) and shows three things:
the flow exists forever, it carries enough curvature information to
produce explicit shrinking holomorphic coordinates near every point, and
those coordinates can be glued into one global biholomorphism onto $\C^n$.

\section*{How to read this paper}
Three reading paths:

\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{One hour.} Read this page, the notation table, the road map,
  Section~\ref{sec:introduction}~(\S\ref{sec:structure}), and the
  statements of Theorem~\ref{thm:uniformization},
  Theorem~\ref{thm:localized-flow},
  Theorem~\ref{thm:joint-positivity},
  Proposition~\ref{prop:shrinking-charts},
  Proposition~\ref{prop:polynomial-models},
  Proposition~\ref{prop:global-coordinates}.
\item \emph{One day.} Add every theorem, proposition, lemma, and
  corollary statement, in order. Every proof can be skipped on this path.
\item \emph{Checking the argument.} Every definition, statement and proof
  in the paper. All numerical claims are stated; no hidden inputs.
\end{itemize}

The three time variables play distinct roles and are easy to confuse:
\[
\begin{array}{l|l|l}
\text{clock} & \text{definition} & \text{role}\\\hline
\text{physical} & t & \text{K\"ahler--Ricci flow}\\
\text{logarithmic} & \tau=\log t & \text{joint positivity, Harnack}\\
\text{volume} & s=\rho(o,t) & \text{chart contraction, degree bounds}
\end{array}
\]
In Sections~\ref{sec:discs}--\ref{sec:variation} the letter $s$ is
temporarily reused for the physical time at a disc center. From
Section~\ref{sec:charts} onward it always means the volume clock and
nothing else.

% =============================================================================
\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}

In his 1982 survey, Yau asked whether a complete noncompact K\"ahler
manifold with positive holomorphic bisectional curvature must be
biholomorphic to complex Euclidean space
\cite[\S9(b), p.~45]{YauProblems}. We prove this, with no additional
hypotheses.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:uniformization}
Let $M$ be a connected noncompact complex manifold of complex dimension
$n\ge 1$. If $M$ carries a smooth complete K\"ahler metric with strictly
positive holomorphic bisectional curvature, then $M$ is biholomorphic
to $\C^n$.
\end{theorem}

Strict positivity is pointwise: no uniform lower bound, no upper
curvature bound, no volume-growth, noncollapsing, Ricci-pinching, or
topological hypothesis is added. The conclusion is biholomorphism onto
\emph{all} of $\C^n$, not onto a proper domain, and it does not claim
the given metric is Euclidean. In particular, $M$ is Stein and
contractible.

\subsection{Antecedents and the remaining analytic difficulties}
\label{sec:antecedents}

In one complex dimension, the result is classical: a complete
noncompact Riemann surface with positive Gaussian curvature is
conformally the plane, by the Cohn--Vossen topology theorem and the
Blanc--Fiala--Huber parabolicity theorem
\cite[Satz~8]{CohnVossen}\cite[Theorem~15]{Huber}.

In the compact case, the Frankel conjecture was proved by Mori and by
Siu--Yau: positive holomorphic bisectional curvature characterizes
complex projective space \cite{MoriAmple,SiuYauFrankel}. Yau's
noncompact question \cite[\S9(b), p.~45]{YauProblems} asks for the
analogous characterization of $\C^n$, allowing arbitrary geometry at
infinity.

\emph{Approach via functions and embeddings.}
Greene--Wu showed that positive real sectional curvature yields a smooth
strictly convex exhaustion, hence Steinness
\cite[Theorem~10]{GreeneWuConvex1976}. Mok embedded the manifold into
an affine-algebraic variety under positive bisectional curvature,
maximal volume growth, and quadratic scalar-curvature decay
\cite{MokEmbedding1984}. Chen--Zhu proved intrinsic growth and
average-curvature estimates under pointwise positive bisectional
curvature, with a linear average-scalar-curvature bound whose constant
may depend on the center \cite{ChenZhu}; they also studied algebraic
compactification under bounded sectional curvature and a finite top
Ricci integral \cite{ChenZhuPositive2009}. Ni--Tam studied heat
deformation of plurisubharmonic functions under nonnegative bisectional
curvature, keeping an explicit growth condition on the initial
function \cite{NiTamStructure}.

\emph{Approach via geometric flow.}
Cao's differential Harnack inequalities for K\"ahler--Ricci flow
\cite{CaoHarnack} underlie everything below. Chau--Tam established
uniformization under bounded nonnegative bisectional curvature and
maximal volume growth \cite{ChauTamComplex2006}, and later developed
locally biholomorphic charts, covering and pseudoconvexity, and
soliton-limit methods under further decay or pinching assumptions
\cite{ChauTamStein,ChauTamSimply}. The difference between a covering,
a map onto a domain, and a biholomorphism onto $\C^n$ matters here.
Liu proved uniformization under nonnegative bisectional curvature and
maximal volume growth, without upper curvature bound, via a
Gromov--Hausdorff argument that retracts holomorphic vector fields
\cite{LiuUniformization}; Lee--Tam later obtained the same conclusion
by smoothing the metric and applying the bounded-curvature theorem
\cite{LeeTam}. Both retain maximal volume growth.

\emph{Current frontier.}
Recent work removes assumptions at infinity in special cases.\footnote{%
All cited versions record AI assistance. Datar--Pingali--Seshadri credit
ChatGPT Pro~5.2 for the normalization observation in Lemma~5.13
\cite[\S1.1, Acknowledgments]{DatarPingaliSeshadri}, and ChatGPT~5.5~Pro
for the auxiliary function and induction in the B\'ezout estimates,
plus ChatGPT~5.0 in thinking mode for Lemma~2.2
\cite[\S\S1--3]{DatarPingaliSeshadriTop}. Wu reports ChatGPT-6
assistance, with author verification
\cite[Acknowledgments]{WuConditional}.}
Datar--Pingali--Seshadri \cite[Theorem~1]{DatarPingaliSeshadri} prove
that every complete noncompact K\"ahler surface with positive real
sectional curvature is biholomorphic to $\C^2$, via Lipschitz
plurisubharmonic weights of finite Monge--Amp\`ere mass and weighted
holomorphic functions; their Theorem~2 gives the same for positive
bisectional curvature under strong Steinness, contractibility and
simple connectivity at infinity. Wu
\cite[Corollaries~1.5--1.6]{WuConditional} removes contractibility,
retaining strong Steinness and simple connectivity at infinity; he also
handles positive bisectional curvature when real sectional curvatures
are nonnegative outside a compact set. These are surface results, and
positive real sectional curvature is stronger than positive bisectional
curvature.

In higher dimensions, Datar--Pingali--Seshadri
\cite[Theorem~1.1]{DatarPingaliSeshadriTop} prove finiteness of the
integral of the top power of the Ricci form on complete noncompact
K\"ahler manifolds with positive real sectional curvature, and the same
under positive bisectional curvature given a smooth strictly
plurisubharmonic exhaustion with uniformly bounded gradient, with
quasiprojectivity under bounded positive sectional curvature
\cite[Theorem~1.2]{DatarPingaliSeshadriTop}. Dat \cite{DatHessian}
develops an $m$-Hessian capacity approach in complex dimension $\ge3$;
his uniformization route retains proper-map and multiplicity
assumptions. Shi \cite[Theorem~1.6]{ShiMinimalDegrees} proves an
identity between the minimal degree of holomorphic volume forms and
refined minimal degrees of holomorphic functions for complete
noncompact K\"ahler manifolds with nonnegative bisectional curvature and
nonsplitting universal cover, tying the function-theoretic and flow
pictures together in the maximal-growth regime
\cite[Theorem~1.7, Corollary~1.12]{ShiMinimalDegrees}.

\emph{Two obstructions in the unrestricted problem.}
First, the initial metric can have unbounded curvature and arbitrarily
collapsed regions, so a bounded-curvature flow theorem or a global
tensor maximum principle cannot be invoked before its hypotheses are
established. Second, shrinking charts alone identify $M$ with a proper
domain of $\C^n$; surjectivity requires quantitative control of the
chart changes even when contraction rates vary substantially.

\subsection{Structure of the proof}\label{sec:structure}

Fix $o\in M$. We construct a global K\"ahler--Ricci flow $h_t$ by
complete Hermitian approximations on exhausting domains. Estimates for
$h_t$ and for the volume-loss potential
$\rho(x,t)=\log(\det g(x)/\det h_t(x))$ pass to the limit without a
global curvature bound. A spatial weight adapted to the nonnegative
form $g-h_t$ proves scalar comparison before completeness of $h_t$ is
known. Order matters here: completeness of positive-time slices is a
\emph{consequence} of the Harnack inequality, not an assumption.

The curvature argument is on the holomorphic cotangent bundle. Minimize
the boundary squared dual norm over holomorphic discs, with compact
boundary walls and a vanishing area penalty. The canonical symplectic
bivector relates the complex Hessian of the norm to its time derivative.
A quantitative deformation lemma gives a viscosity inequality for the
disc infimum; optimizing a Hermitian ellipsoid in each fiber converts it
into the scalar comparison already proved. The infimum therefore equals
the evolving squared dual norm, and $N(x,\xi,e^{w+\bar w})$ is jointly
plurisubharmonic in space and logarithmic time. That yields the scalar
and matrix Harnack inequalities.

The volume clock $s=\rho(o,t)$ measures contraction directly. For a
transition $F_{s,u}$ between centered unitary charts at clocks $s$ and
$u\ge s$,
\[
 \abs{\det F_{s,u}'(0)}=e^{-(u-s)/2}.
\]
Writing $R(x,t)$ for the scalar curvature of $h_t$, the speed of the
volume clock in logarithmic time is $q(s)=ds/d\tau=tR(o,t)$. A static
logarithmic-norm estimate bounds the greatest singular length of an
$h_t$-unitary local chart, measured in the initial metric $g$, by a
fixed power of its least singular length; together with the
determinant loss, this controls contraction in every direction.
Selected time intervals with Ricci pinching then give a loop
contraction argument. Simple connectivity lets the local inverse charts
continue coherently over exhausting compact sets, and a
plurisubharmonic Liouville argument gives one contraction exponent
valid on every fixed compact set.

Finally, normalize the volume jets of the transition maps and build
polynomial automorphisms of constant Jacobian. The nonlinear shears used
to realize normalized jets have determinant one, so the full models
retain a contracting linear determinant. Intervals of nonuniform
contraction are permitted; their degree cost is charged to growth of
$q$ in the volume clock. Backward iteration of these models makes the
corrected inverse charts converge to an injective holomorphic map
$M\to\C^n$. A subsequential degree bound for the forward compositions,
together with smallness on compact subsets of the image and an
inverse-ball inclusion, eliminates a finite maximal radius for centered
balls in the image. Hence the limiting coordinate map is onto $\C^n$.

Sections~\ref{sec:preliminaries}--\ref{sec:flow} build the analytic
data and prove scalar comparison. Sections~\ref{sec:discs}--\ref{sec:ellipsoids}
prove joint positivity via discs and ellipsoids. Section~\ref{sec:charts}
obtains completeness, simple connectivity and quantitative shrinking
charts. Sections~\ref{sec:dynamics}--\ref{sec:uniformization} construct
the biholomorphism. The disc-deformation estimate and the forward-degree
estimate are stated with exactly the uniformities their later
applications need.

\subsection{Conventions}\label{sec:conventions}

We use the real curvature convention in which $\Rm(a,b,b,a)$ is the
sectional-curvature numerator of the real plane spanned by $a,b$. The
hypothesis is
\[
 \Rm(u,v,v,u)+\Rm(u,Jv,Jv,u)>0
\]
for every pair of real unit tangent vectors at every point. By the
K\"ahler symmetries this is equivalent to strict Griffiths positivity of
$T^{1,0}M$ in complex notation. In complex dimension one the displayed
sum is the Gaussian curvature.

We identify a Hermitian tensor $(a_{i\bar j})$ with its real
$(1,1)$-form, dropping the factor $\sqrt{-1}$ when no confusion can
result. Thus $\dd f$ is the complex Hessian, $\Delta_h f=\tr_h\dd f$,
and
\[
 \partial_t h_t=-\Ric(h_t),\qquad
 \Ric(h)=-\dd\log\det h.
\]
The dual of a Hermitian metric includes the transpose dictated by the
holomorphic cotangent pairing; matrix contractions retain it explicitly
where needed.

The initial complete metric is $g$, the base point is $o$, and
$r(x)=d_g(o,x)$. Constants may change between estimates; subscripts
record dependencies when those matter. For Hermitian forms,
$A=O(b)h$ means $-Cbh\le A\le Cbh$. The abbreviation psh means
plurisubharmonic. A positive constant extracted from curvature on a
specified compact set is never uniform over all of $M$.

% End of front matter and introduction. Next sections appended below.

% =============================================================================
\section{Functions, sections, and smoothing on the initial manifold}
\label{sec:preliminaries}

Throughout this section, distances, balls, norms, and volumes are those
of the complete initial metric $g$. Fix $o\in M$ and put $r(x)=\dist_g(o,x)$.
Constants may depend on $g$ and $o$, but not uniformly on the center.

We need four pieces of analytic data before we can run a flow or study
holomorphic discs. First, a strictly plurisubharmonic exhaustion with
controlled gradient and complex Hessian; second, finitely many global
holomorphic functions and vector fields that detect every tangent
direction; third, global holomorphic canonical sections with linear
logarithmic growth; fourth, a smoothing procedure that produces a
two-sided complex Hessian bound on distance truncations even when no
curvature upper bound is available. Those make up
Proposition~\ref{prop:initial-data} and the lemmas of the two
subsections below.

\begin{proposition}[Initial analytic data]\label{prop:initial-data}
The following objects and estimates exist.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item A smooth strictly plurisubharmonic $\psi$ with $\psi\le C(1+r)$.
\item Holomorphic functions and holomorphic tangent fields that
      interpolate arbitrary finite jets at a finite set of points.
      In particular, finitely many holomorphic functions define an
      injective immersion $F:M\to\C^N$, and finitely many global
      holomorphic tangent fields span $T^{1,0}M$ pointwise.
\item At every $x\in M$ there is a global holomorphic canonical section
      $S$ with $S(x)\ne0$ and $\log|S|_g^2\le C_S(1+r)$. There is also a
      smooth $\Xi$ with
      \begin{equation}\label{prelim:xi}
      \dd\Xi\ge\Ric(g),\qquad \Xi\le C(1+r^{3/2}).
      \end{equation}
\item A smooth exhaustion $u\ge1$ satisfying
      \begin{equation}\label{prelim:exhaustion}
      C^{-1}(1+r)\le u\le C(1+r),\qquad
      |\partial u|_g\le C,\qquad |\dd u|_g\le C/u.
      \end{equation}
\item If $S(o)\ne0$ is chosen as in \textup{(iii)}, then for $L\ge1$,
      \begin{equation}\label{prelim:averages}
      \frac1{\Vol B(o,L)}\int_{B(o,L)}\bigl|\log|S|_g^2\bigr|\,dV_g
      \le CL,\qquad
      \frac1{\Vol B(o,L)}\int_{B(o,L)}R_g\,dV_g\le C/L.
      \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
Neither $F$ nor $\psi$ is proper. The exhaustion in \textup{(iv)} has a
two-sided complex Hessian bound, which is what the flow arguments
actually use.
\end{proposition}

The rest of this section proves the proposition. Subsection~\ref{sec:weight}
constructs $\psi$ (via a Busemann function) and the holomorphic
interpolation data. Subsection~\ref{sec:hodge} proves the Hodge-heat
order and commutation facts needed later. Subsection~\ref{sec:dyadic}
combines both to obtain the dyadic exhaustion and the averaged bounds.

\subsection{The weight and holomorphic interpolation}\label{sec:weight}

Let $\gamma$ be a unit-speed ray starting at $o$ and set
\[
 b(x)=\lim_{j\to\infty}\bigl\{d_g(o,\gamma(j))-d_g(x,\gamma(j))\bigr\}.
\]
The triangle inequality gives monotonicity in braces, uniform boundedness
on compact sets, and $|b(x)-b(y)|\le d_g(x,y)$.

\paragraph{Busemann is strictly plurisubharmonic.}
Fix $x$ in a compact set and a minimizing segment of length $l$ from $x$
to $\gamma(j)$. For an initial vector $X$, use its parallel translate
multiplied by $1-\sigma/l$ as a comparison variation field, and the same
construction for $JX$. The second variation of length, computed on
their perpendicular components, has curvature contribution the negative
of
\[
 \int_0^l\Bigl(1-\tfrac{\sigma}{l}\Bigr)^2
 \bigl\{\Rm(\dot\gamma,X_\parallel,X_\parallel,\dot\gamma)
        +\Rm(\dot\gamma,JX_\parallel,JX_\parallel,\dot\gamma)\bigr\}
 \,d\sigma,
\]
which is a positive multiple of the bisectional curvature of the complex
two-plane spanned by $\dot\gamma$ and $X$. Removing radial components
does not change the expression. On a fixed short initial subsegment,
positivity is bounded below uniformly in the unit initial vector
$X$ and the base point in the chosen compact. The derivative term in
the index form is $O(l^{-1})$. Hence
\[
 \partial^2_{X,\bar X}\!d_g(\cdot,\gamma(j))
 + \partial^2_{JX,\overline{JX}}\!d_g(\cdot,\gamma(j))
 \le -c_K|X|^2 + O(l^{-1})|X|^2
\]
with $c_K>0$ independent of $j$.

This comparison does not need a smooth distance function. Vary the
initial endpoint along a holomorphic germ and use the comparison paths
to obtain smooth upper supports for distance. The sum of the endpoint
acceleration terms vanishes for the two real directions of that germ,
so their negatives give lower supports for the signed distance with the
same strict Levi lower bound. An upper test of the signed distance also
lies above its lower support and therefore inherits that bound. Local
uniform passage to $b$ preserves the inequality. Testing on complex
lines gives the subharmonic version. Hence $b$ is strictly
plurisubharmonic, with a positive Levi lower bound on every compact.
Richberg approximation with pointwise error less than one gives a smooth
strictly plurisubharmonic $\psi$ with $\psi\le1+r$, after adding a
constant \cite{Richberg}\cite[Section~4, Corollary~2]{GreeneWu}.

\paragraph{Holomorphic interpolation.}
Here are the bundle choices. Let $K=\bigwedge^nT^{*(1,0)}M$. A function,
a canonical section, and a tangent field correspond respectively to a
holomorphic top form with coefficients in
\begin{equation}\label{prelim:coefficient-bundles}
 K^{-1},\qquad \boldone,\qquad T^{1,0}M\otimes K^{-1}.
\end{equation}
The first has nonnegative curvature; the second is flat; the third has
nonnegative Nakano curvature. Indeed it is $E\otimes\det E$ with
$E=T^{1,0}M$ Griffiths-positive, so the Demailly--Skoda
tensor-with-determinant theorem applies \cite[Theorem~2.6]{Demailly}.
These statements concern the bundles in \eqref{prelim:coefficient-bundles};
no Nakano positivity of $E$ itself is being assumed.

Prescribe jets of order $m$ at a finite set $A$. Choose disjoint
coordinate balls and local holomorphic sections with those jets,
multiply them by cutoffs equal to one on smaller balls, obtaining a
smooth section $s_0$ with $\dbar s_0$ supported in a compact set
disjoint from $A$. Use the weight
\[
 k\psi+\sum_{a\in A}c_m\vartheta_a\log|z_a|^2,\qquad c_m>n+m,
\]
where each $\vartheta_a$ is one near $a$ and supported in its coordinate
ball. The negative Hessian terms of the cutoff logarithms are supported
in a fixed compact, and a sufficiently large $k$ makes the total weight
strictly plurisubharmonic and dominant there. The inverse-curvature norm
of $\dbar s_0$ is integrable because its support avoids the poles. The
complete K\"ahler $L^2$ estimate for bundle-valued top forms gives
$v$ with $\dbar v=\dbar s_0$ and finite weighted norm
\cite{Hormander}\cite[Theorem~5.1]{Demailly}. The singular-weight
statement follows by regularizing the logarithms and taking a weak
limit. Near a point of $A$, $v$ is holomorphic, and integrability against
$|z_a|^{-2c_m}$ forces its Taylor coefficients through order $m$ to
vanish. Hence $s_0-v$ has the prescribed jets. Applying this to pairs of
points proves point separation.

For a canonical section the same construction yields
\[
 \int_M |S|_g^2e^{-k\psi}\,dV_g<\infty.
\]
The cutoff pole weights can be dropped at the cost of a constant.
$|S|_g^2$ is subharmonic: away from its zeros,
$\dd\log|S|_g^2=\Ric(g)$, and across zeros its squared norm still has
nonnegative distributional Laplacian. The local mean-value inequality on
a unit ball \cite[Theorem~2.1]{LiSchoen}, combined with the upper bound
for $\psi$, gives
\[
 |S(x)|_g^2\le\frac{C_S}{\Vol B(x,1)}\exp(C_S(1+r(x))).
\]
Nonnegative Ricci curvature gives
$\Vol B(x,1)\ge c(1+r(x))^{-2n}$ via Bishop--Gromov relative volume
comparison \cite[\S2.1]{GromovBetti}. The polynomial loss is absorbed by
the exponential. This proves \textup{(iii)} and the growth assertion,
matching the canonical-section construction in
\cite[Section~3]{ChenZhu}.

\paragraph{Finite tuples.}
Why finitely many functions and fields suffice is a dimension count, not
an approximation argument. The compact-open spaces of holomorphic
functions and sections are Fr\'echet, hence Baire. Fix a compact family
of points, or of distinct pairs of points. Surjectivity of
finite-jet evaluation, openness of matrix rank, and a finite cover give
a finite-dimensional space of perturbations on which the evaluation
derivative is onto on a neighborhood of that compact family; the
derivative is independent of the perturbation parameter, because
evaluations are affine. Therefore the universal evaluation is a
submersion there.

For each smooth stratum of the forbidden locus, its inverse image under
the evaluation has real codimension equal to that of the stratum. If
that codimension exceeds the real dimension of the point or pair space,
the inverse image has dimension less than the perturbation space. Its
projection has measure zero: cover by countably many compact coordinate
patches, use Lipschitz projection on each, and cover a $d$-dimensional
cube by $O(\delta^{-d})$ small cubes to see the image in $\R^p$, $p>d$,
has volume at most $C\delta^{p-d}$.

For $N$ functions the rank-$r$ stratum of $N\times n$ derivative
matrices has complex codimension $(N-r)(n-r)\ge N-n+1$ when $r<n$.
For $N\ge2n+1$ this exceeds $n$. The equal-values condition on distinct
pairs has complex codimension $N>2n$. Avoidance on a compact set is
open in the compact-open topology via Cauchy estimates. A countable
exhaustion of $M$ and of $M\times M\setminus\operatorname{diag}$, then
Baire, gives one finite injective immersion. For $m$ tangent sections
the least deficient-rank codimension is $m-n+1>n$ when $m\ge2n$; the
same zeroth-jet argument gives a finite spanning family. Neither is
claimed proper.

\paragraph{The function $\Xi$ in \textup{(iii)}.}
Choose countably many canonical sections $S_i$ with no common zero.
Write $a_i=|S_i|_g^2$; the bound $a_i\le\exp(C_i(1+r))$ implies
\[
 a_i\le e^{B_i}\exp(1+r^{3/2}).
\]
Choose $c_i>0$ so small that $c_ie^{B_i}\le2^{-i}$ and every derivative
of $c_ia_i$ of order at most $i$ has norm at most $2^{-i}$ on the first
$i$ exhaustion compacts. The sum $\sum c_ia_i$ converges smoothly
locally, is positive, and is bounded above by $\exp(1+r^{3/2})$. In a
local canonical frame it is $\det(g)^{-1}$ times a sum of squares of
holomorphic functions. Hence $\Xi=\log\sum_i c_i|S_i|_g^2$ satisfies
\eqref{prelim:xi}.

\subsection{Hodge heat without a curvature upper bound}\label{sec:hodge}

To smooth distance truncations later, we need to preserve an upper
bound on their complex Hessian. Scalar heat estimates then control the
trace and give the missing lower Hessian bound. We record here how heat
evolution of a bounded closed $(1,1)$-form produces a scalar potential
for its change. That identity will be used again when constructing the
flow comparison cutoff.

Let $H_a$ be scalar heat for $\Delta_g$. Normalize the Hodge heat
operator $\mathcal H_a$ on real $(1,1)$-forms to agree with $H_a$
after taking trace, and normalize so that its generator on closed real
$(1,1)$-forms is $A\mapsto \dd\tr_g A$.

\paragraph{Mass of the scalar heat kernel.}
Fix a pole $y$ and let $\chi_R$ be a Lipschitz distance cutoff equal to
one on $B_g(y,R)$, supported in $B_g(y,2R)$, with $|\nabla\chi_R|\le
C/R$. The Gaussian estimate of Li--Yau \cite{LiYau} and the kernel
gradient estimate of Souplet--Zhang \cite[Theorem~1.3]{SoupletZhang},
together with Bishop--Gromov comparison \cite[\S2.1]{GromovBetti}, give
\[
 \int_{R\le d(x,y)\le2R}|\nabla_xH_s(x,y)|\,dV_g(x)
 \le Cs^{-1/2}(1+R/\sqrt s)^N e^{-cR^2/s}
\]
for a fixed dimensional exponent $N$. Integrating the heat equation
against $\chi_R$ from $\varepsilon$ to $t\le T$ and letting
$\varepsilon\downarrow0$ yields
\[
 \Bigl|\int_M\chi_R(x)H_t(x,y)\,dV_g(x)-1\Bigr|
 \le\frac CR\int_0^T s^{-1/2}
  (1+R/\sqrt s)^N e^{-cR^2/s}\,ds,
\]
and the right side tends to zero as $R\to\infty$. Positivity and the
sub-Markov property of the minimal kernel allow passage to the total
mass, so $H_t1=1$. The cutoffs only need completeness and the first
derivative of distance; they precede the smooth exhaustion below.

\begin{lemma}[Heat order and commutation]\label{lem:hodge-heat}
For bounded real $(1,1)$-forms, Hodge heat is well defined by cutoff
limits of the complete $L^2$ semigroup. It preserves order and
\[
 -Cg\le A\le Cg\ \Longrightarrow\
 -Cg\le\mathcal H_aA\le Cg,\qquad
 \tr_g\mathcal H_aA=H_a\tr_gA.
\]
If $A$ is also smooth and closed, then
\begin{equation}\label{prelim:hodge-identity}
 \mathcal H_aA=A+\dd\int_0^aH_b\tr_gA\,db.
\end{equation}
If a Lipschitz $f$ is constant outside a compact set and $\dd f\le Cg$
as currents, then
\begin{equation}\label{prelim:hessian-preservation}
 \dd H_af\le Cg\qquad(a>0).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For smooth compactly supported data, take the componentwise
zero-Dirichlet Hodge heat on a relatively compact smooth domain. Its
Weitzenb\"ock formula is a connection Laplacian plus a zeroth-order
curvature reaction. At a null vector $X$ of a nonnegative Hermitian
form $A$, diagonalize $A$ in a unitary frame; the reaction on
$(X,\bar X)$ is a positive fixed multiple of
\[
 \sum_j R_g(X,\bar X,e_j,\bar e_j)A_{j\bar j}\ge0
\]
and the Ricci-times-$A$ terms vanish there. The tensor maximum principle
preserves the nonnegative cone. Apply the same argument to $Cg-A$ to
get the upper bound. The trace solves the scalar heat equation with
zero boundary condition. This is the null-eigenvector mechanism of
\cite[Section~2]{NiTamStructure}.

\emph{Complete realization and exhaustion.} On compactly supported
smooth forms put $D=d+d^*$. Completeness gives essential self-adjointness
of $D^2$, hence of $D$: a deficiency vector for $D$ would lie in
$\ker((D^2)^*+1)=0$
\cite[Corollary~2.10, Remark~2.12]{BravermanMilatovicShubin}. The
quadratic form of the resulting Hodge Laplacian is
\[
 q(A)=\|dA\|_2^2+\|d^*A\|_2^2,\qquad
 V=\overline{C^\infty_c}^{\,(\|\cdot\|_2^2+q)^{1/2}}.
\]
For a nested smooth exhaustion $\Omega_j$, let $V_j$ be the same
closure of forms supported in $\Omega_j$, extended by zero. On a fixed
domain, local ellipticity identifies this with the full zero
boundary-value section domain (not an absolute or relative Hodge
condition). The $V_j$ are nested and their union is dense in $V$. For
$\lambda>0$ and an $L^2$ form, the domain resolvent applied to it and
extended by zero is the orthogonal projection of the complete resolvent
onto $V_j$ for the inner product $q+\lambda\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle$:
both satisfy the same variational identity against $V_j$. Hence the
projections converge in that norm. Strong resolvent convergence and the
common lower bound zero give strong heat convergence
\cite[Section~6.6, Theorem~6.31, Corollary~6.33]{Teschl2009}. Here
$\Delta_j$ denotes the nonnegative domain Hodge Laplacian; zero
extension of its heat means $e^{-a\Delta_j}P_j$, with $P_j$ restriction
followed by zero-extension of the generator.

\emph{Cone and trace passes to the limit.} The complete Hodge semigroup
has the off-diagonal estimate
\begin{equation}\label{prelim:off-diagonal}
 \|\boldone_E\mathcal H_a\boldone_F\|_{2\to2}
 \le C\exp(-c\dist(E,F)^2/a).
\end{equation}
This needs no curvature upper bound: use the self-adjoint first-order
operator $d+d^*$, whose principal symbol has norm equal to the covector
norm, so the wave evolution has finite propagation speed
\cite[Theorem~1.1, Section~4]{McIntoshMorris}. The Gaussian Fourier
representation of $\exp(-a(d+d^*)^2)$ then gives
\eqref{prelim:off-diagonal}, up to the constants in our normalization
\cite[Theorem~3.4]{CoulhonSikora}. Since $\Vol B(o,R)\le CR^{2n}$,
dyadic annular decomposition makes the cutoffs of a bounded form
Cauchy in $L^2$ on each compact, uniformly for bounded positive times.
Interior parabolic estimates give smooth convergence away from $a=0$.

\emph{Closedness.} Use the complete semigroup, not the Dirichlet
operators on domains. Let $\zeta_R$ be a compactly supported Lipschitz
cutoff equal to one on $B(o,R)$, supported on $B(o,2R)$ with
$|d\zeta_R|\le C/R$. The complete Hodge semigroup commutes with $d$ on
the $L^2$ domains, by the self-adjoint de Rham complex, and
$De^{-aD^2}=e^{-aD^2}D$ gives the $d$ identity in degree one higher.
The data $\zeta_RA$ belong to the full $D$ graph domain by compactly
supported $H^1$ approximation. For bounded closed $A$,
\[
 d\mathcal H_a(\zeta_RA)=\mathcal H_a^{(3)}(d\zeta_R\wedge A).
\]
The data on the right have $L^2$ norm at most $CR^{n-1}$ and support
outside $B(o,R)$; \eqref{prelim:off-diagonal} in degree three makes them
tend to zero on each compact, uniformly for $0<a\le a_0$. Distributional
passage to the limit gives $d\mathcal H_aA=0$, and local regularity
gives its initial value $A$. K\"ahler commutation on closed forms now
gives
\[
 \partial_a\mathcal H_aA=\dd\tr_g\mathcal H_aA=\dd H_a\tr_gA.
\]
Integration proves \eqref{prelim:hodge-identity}, first distributionally
then as smooth forms for $a>0$. The same passage from closed-form heat
to a scalar potential via its trace is the mechanism in
\cite[Lemma~2.1, proof of Theorem~2.1]{NiTamHodge2013}.

\emph{Hessian preservation.} Put $f_0=f-c$ with $c$ the value at
infinity; this is compactly supported and Lipschitz. Complete $L^2$
K\"ahler commutation, smooth approximation, and duality give, for a
compactly supported nonnegative Hermitian test form $B$,
\[
 \langle\dd H_af_0,B\rangle=\langle\dd f_0,\mathcal H_aB\rangle.
\]
The metric pairing identifies the cone of nonnegative Hermitian forms
with its dual; equivalently one pairs with the Hodge star. Choose
$0\le\zeta\le1$ compactly supported and equal to one near
$\supp f_0$. Since $\dd f_0$ is supported there,
\[
 \langle\dd f_0,\mathcal H_aB\rangle
 =\langle\dd f_0,\zeta\mathcal H_aB\rangle
 \le C\int\zeta\tr_g\mathcal H_aB\,dV_g
 \le C\int\tr_gB\,dV_g.
\]
The last two steps use positivity and scalar trace commutation. Since
$H_a1=1$ under nonnegative Ricci curvature, $\dd H_af=\dd H_af_0$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Dyadic smoothing and averaged curvature}\label{sec:dyadic}

For $L=2^j$, $j\ge0$, choose a smooth nondecreasing scalar truncation of
$r^2/L^2$ which vanishes for $r\le L$ and equals one for $r\ge2L$, and
call it $f_L$. It is Lipschitz with constant $C/L$. The paired
index-form comparison with linear parallel fields gives
$\dd r^2\le Cg$ away from the cut locus, and in the upper-support sense
at the cut locus. The derivative-square term from the truncation is
bounded on its annulus. Hence $\dd f_L\le CL^{-2}g$ as currents. (The
word smooth describes the scalar truncation; $f_L$ itself may not be
smooth at the cut locus.)

Let $w_L=H_{\epsilon L^2}f_L$ for a fixed small $\epsilon>0$. Gaussian
heat bounds and the Lipschitz estimate give
\[
 \|w_L-f_L\|_\infty\le(C/L)\sup_x\int d_g(x,y)H_{\epsilon L^2}(x,y)
 dV_g(y)\le C\sqrt{\epsilon}.
\]
Nonnegative Ricci curvature suffices for these scalar heat estimates.
The Li--Yau gradient inequality, applied to $1+H_af_L$ and $2-H_af_L$,
gives at $a=\epsilon L^2$
\[
 |\Delta_gw_L|\le C_\epsilon L^{-2},\qquad
 |\partial w_L|_g\le C_\epsilon L^{-1}.
\]
The first translate bounds $\partial_aH_af_L$ below, and the second
bounds it above; substituting both in the same inequality bounds the
gradient; see \cite[Theorem~1.3(i)]{LiYau}.
Lemma~\ref{lem:hodge-heat} gives the upper bound for each complex
Hessian eigenvalue; the lower trace bound then gives the lower bound
for each. Thus $|\dd w_L|_g\le C_\epsilon L^{-2}$.

Choose a smooth nondecreasing step $\theta$, zero near zero and one
near one, with constant regions wide enough to contain the above heat
error. Then $v_L=\theta(w_L)$ vanishes on $r\le L$, equals one on
$r\ge2L$, and
\[
 |\partial v_L|_g\le C/L,\qquad |\dd v_L|_g\le C/L^2.
\]
The locally finite sum
\[
 u=1+\sum_{j\ge0}2^jv_{2^j}
\]
is comparable to $1+r$, and at any point only a bounded number of
summands have nonzero derivatives, at scales comparable to $1+r$. This
proves \eqref{prelim:exhaustion}. Scalar cutoffs $p(u/L)$ have gradient
$O(L^{-1})$ and absolute complex Hessian $O(L^{-2})$, with support and
transition region in fixed multiples of $B(o,L)$.

\paragraph{Averaged curvature and averaged section norm.}
Put $w=\log|S|_g^2$ for a canonical section with $S(o)\ne0$. The
Poincar\'e--Lelong formula gives
\begin{equation}\label{prelim:canonical-current}
 \dd w\ge\Ric(g)
\end{equation}
as currents. The truncations $w_m=\max(w,-m)$ are subharmonic. The heat
submean inequality and linear upper growth give
\[
 w_m(o)\le H_{L^2}w_m(o),\qquad H_{L^2}(w_m)^+(o)\le CL.
\]
Justify the submean first on exhaustion domains and then by Gaussian
decay; the positive part grows at most linearly. For $m$ large,
$w_m(o)=w(o)$, so $H_{L^2}(w_m)^-(o)\le CL+|w(o)|$. The heat-kernel
lower bound on $B(o,L)$ is $c/\Vol B(o,L)$. Monotone convergence as
$m\to\infty$ gives the first estimate of \eqref{prelim:averages}.

Take a nonnegative cutoff equal to one on $B(o,L)$ and supported in
$B(o,CL)$, with absolute Laplacian at most $C/L^2$, as just constructed.
Integrate the trace of \eqref{prelim:canonical-current} against it and
move $\Delta_g$ onto the cutoff:
\[
 \int_{B(o,L)}R_g\,dV_g\le\frac{C}{L^2}\int_{B(o,CL)}|w|\,dV_g
 \le\frac CL\Vol B(o,L).
\]
The last step uses volume doubling. This proves the second estimate.
The average-scalar-curvature conclusion is
\cite[Theorem~2]{ChenZhu}; the details above record the specific
cutoffs and the absolute logarithmic estimate used later.

% =============================================================================
\section{A global flow and scalar comparison by localization}
\label{sec:flow}

We first construct the flow with estimates on fixed initial compact
sets. Completeness of its positive-time slices is a consequence of the
Harnack inequality, proved later in
Proposition~\ref{prop:harnack-completeness}.

\begin{theorem}[Localized flow]\label{thm:localized-flow}
There is a smooth K\"ahler--Ricci flow $h_t$ on $M$ for $0\le t<\infty$,
with $h_0=g$. There are smooth real functions $U,P,\rho$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{flow:identities}
 \begin{aligned}
 0<h_t\le g,\qquad
 h_t=g-t\Ric(g)+\dd U,\qquad U(\cdot,0)=0,\\
 P=U_t=\log\frac{\det h_t}{\det g},\qquad
 \rho=-P\ge0,\qquad R_{h_t}=-P_t\ge0.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For every finite $T$ there is $C_T$ with
\begin{equation}\label{flow:rho-average}
 \sup_{0\le t\le T}\frac1{\Vol_gB_g(o,L)}
  \int_{B_g(o,L)}\rho(x,t)\,dV_g(x)\le C_TL\qquad(L\ge1).
\end{equation}
At every fixed $x$, $\rho(x,t)\le C_x(1+t)$ for all $t\ge0$. On each
finite time interval there exist a smooth positive function $Q$ and a
constant $c>0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{flow:proper-supercaloric}
 Q\ge c(1+r^2),\qquad
 (\partial_t-\Delta_{h_t})Q\ge c\tr_{h_t}g.
\end{equation}
Smoothness at $t=0$ means smoothness with all one-sided time derivatives.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Complete Hermitian approximations}\label{sec:flow:approximations}

Take regular values $L\to\infty$ of the exhaustion $u$ from
Proposition~\ref{prop:initial-data} and put
$\Omega_L=\{u<L\}$. Choose a fixed smooth nonnegative $F$ on $[0,1)$,
zero on $[0,1/2]$, equal to $-2\log(1-v)$ near $v=1$, and set on
$\Omega_L$
\[
 f=F(u/L),\qquad \alpha=e^fg.
\]
The estimates for $u$ imply
\begin{equation}\label{flow:conformal-bounds}
 |\partial f|_\alpha\le C/L,\qquad |\dd f|_\alpha\le C/L^2.
\end{equation}
Near the boundary, $e^f=(1-u/L)^{-2}$, and the factors $(1-u/L)^{-1}$ and
$(1-u/L)^{-2}$ in the first and second derivatives are canceled by the
conformal norm. On the transition region $u\asymp L$, $|\dd u|_g\le C/u$
gives the same.

The metric $\alpha$ is complete on $\Omega_L$: approaching the regular
boundary has infinite length for the factor $(1-u/L)^{-1}$, and the
closure of $\Omega_L$ is compact. For each fixed $L$ it has bounded
geometry of every order in holomorphic charts. Take finitely many
ordinary charts on the compact closure; near a boundary point of
boundary distance comparable to $1-u/L$, shrink their coordinate radii
by a sufficiently small fixed multiple of $1-u/L$. In the rescaled
charts $\alpha$ and its inverse are uniformly bounded, and every
derivative of its coefficients is bounded, with constants allowed to
depend on $L$ and on the derivative order.

Run Chern--Ricci flow on this complete Hermitian background:
\begin{equation}\label{flow:approximation}
 K=\alpha-t\Ric(\alpha)+\dd U,\qquad
 U_t=P=\log\frac{\det K}{\det\alpha},\qquad U(\cdot,0)=0.
\end{equation}
Until the local limit below, $U,P$ refer to this approximation. Bounded-
geometry short-time existence is
\cite[Definition~2.1, Lemma~2.1]{LeeTam}. The estimates we produce hold
on every closed bounded-geometry interval; the resulting continuation
intervals grow with $L$.

\paragraph{Scalar evolutions.}Write $\mathscr L_K=\partial_t-\Delta_K$.
Differentiating \eqref{flow:approximation},
\begin{equation}\label{flow:scalar-evolutions}
 \mathscr L_KP=-\tr_K\Ric(\alpha),\qquad
 \mathscr L_KP_t=-|\Ric(K)|_K^2,
 \qquad P_t(\cdot,0)\le C/L^2.
\end{equation}
The last inequality follows from
$\Ric(\alpha)=\Ric(g)-n\dd f$: initial Ricci positivity plus
\eqref{flow:conformal-bounds}. In particular
\begin{equation}\label{flow:upper-potentials}
 P_t\le C/L^2,\qquad P\le Ct/L^2,\qquad U\le Ct^2/(2L^2).
\end{equation}
These are bounded maximum-principle estimates on an already existing
bounded-geometry interval.

\paragraph{A parameterized barrier.} Fix a horizon $D>0$ and suppose a
smooth $\phi\le0$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{flow:phi-condition}
 \alpha-D\Ric(\alpha)+D\dd\phi\ge c\alpha\qquad(c>0).
\end{equation}
Define
\begin{equation}\label{flow:Q-definition}
 Q=(D-t)P+U-D\phi+nt.
\end{equation}
Using $\mathscr L_KU=P-n+\tr_K(\alpha-t\Ric(\alpha))$ gives the exact
identity
\begin{equation}\label{flow:Q-comparison}
 \mathscr L_KQ=\tr_K\{\alpha-D\Ric(\alpha)+D\dd\phi\}
 \ge c\tr_K\alpha,\qquad Q\ge0.
\end{equation}
The initial value is $-D\phi\ge0$. For bounded $\phi$ the maximum
principle gives the last assertion directly; adding a vanishing positive
multiple of the proper $f$ is an equivalent localization. The same
argument applies to functions with downward logarithmic poles, since
$Q\to+\infty$ there.

Fix $L$ and choose finitely many canonical sections with no common zero
on $\overline{\Omega_L}$. A constant shift of
$\log\sum_i|S_i|_g^2$ is bounded, nonpositive, and has complex Hessian
at least $\Ric(g)$. It satisfies
\[
 \alpha-D\Ric(\alpha)+D\dd\phi\ge\alpha+nD\dd f
 \ge(1-CD/L^2)\alpha,
\]
so for fixed $D$ and large $L$ we may take $c=1/2$. Then
\eqref{flow:upper-potentials} and \eqref{flow:Q-comparison} give both
bounds for $P$ on $0\le t\le D/2$: indeed $(D-t)P\ge D\phi-U-nt$.
Integrating gives both bounds for $U$. These bounds may depend on $L$.

\subsection{The largest metric eigenvalue}\label{sec:flow:eigenvalue}

We next bound the largest eigenvalue of $K$ relative to $\alpha$. The
bound tends to one as $L\to\infty$ wherever $Q$ stays bounded
independently of $L$; the local potential estimates below will supply
that control on each fixed compact set, yielding $h_t\le g$ in the
limit.

At a point, use $g$-normal holomorphic coordinates diagonalizing $K$,
$K_{i\bar i}=\lambda_i$, and suppose $\lambda_1$ is largest. Put
$q=K_{1\bar1}$ and
\[
 W=\frac{K_{1\bar1}}{e^fg_{1\bar1}}.
\]
The Rayleigh quotient in this fixed coordinate direction is a smooth
lower support for the largest relative eigenvalue, equal to it at the
point; it therefore suffices for a maximum-principle test. Wherever
$W\ge1$, direct differentiation gives
\begin{equation}\label{flow:eigenvalue-raw}
 \mathscr L_K\log W
 \le-\frac1q\sum_{i,j}\frac{|\partial_1K_{i\bar j}|^2}{\lambda_i\lambda_j}
 +|\partial\log q|_K^2+CL^{-2}\tr_K\alpha.
\end{equation}

\emph{Derivation of \eqref{flow:eigenvalue-raw}.} Since $K-\alpha$ is
closed,
\[
 \partial_1\partial_{\bar1}K_{i\bar i}-\partial_i\partial_{\bar i}K_{1\bar1}
 =\partial_1\partial_{\bar1}\alpha_{i\bar i}-\partial_i\partial_{\bar i}\alpha_{1\bar1}.
\]
In the chosen coordinates the right side is $(e^f)_{1\bar1}-(e^f)_{i\bar i}$
(the second derivatives of $g$ cancel by K\"ahler symmetry). Dividing
its contraction by $q\ge e^f$ bounds the error by $CL^{-2}\tr_K\alpha$.
Differentiating the denominator $g_{1\bar1}$ contributes
$-\sum_i\lambda_i^{-1}R_g(i,\bar i,1,\bar1)\le0$. The remaining
conformal derivatives have the same bound.

For $i\ne1$, the first-derivative exchange reads
$\partial_1K_{i\bar1}=\partial_iq-e^ff_i$. The $j=1$ terms of the
negative square in \eqref{flow:eigenvalue-raw} dominate
\[
 \Bigl|\partial\log q-W^{-1}\sum_{i\ne1}f_i\,dz_i\Bigr|_K^2.
\]
Expanding this square and using $\partial\log q=\partial\log W+\partial f$
at the point yields
\begin{equation}\label{flow:eigenvalue-gradient}
 \mathscr L_K\log W\le CL^{-2}\tr_K\alpha
 +2W^{-1}|\partial f|_K|\partial\log W|_K.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Penalizing with $Q$.}Set $a=L^{-1}$ and
\[
 J(v)=av+\log(1+av)\qquad(v\ge0).
\]
At a positive maximum of $\log W-J(Q)$,
\[
 W\ge e^{aQ}(1+aQ),\qquad \partial\log W=J'(Q)\partial Q,\quad
 J'\ge a,\quad J'\le2a,\quad -J''=\frac{a^2}{(1+aQ)^2}.
\]
The chain rule, \eqref{flow:Q-comparison} and
\eqref{flow:eigenvalue-gradient} give
\[
 \mathscr L_K(\log W-J(Q))
 \le(CL^{-2}-ca)\tr_K\alpha
 +2W^{-1}J'|\partial f|_K|\partial Q|_K
 +J''|\partial Q|_K^2.
\]
Young's inequality absorbs the middle term into half of
$-J''|\partial Q|_K^2$: its remaining coefficient is bounded by
\[
 C\frac{W^{-2}(J')^2}{-J''}|\partial f|_K^2\le C|\partial f|_K^2\le
 CL^{-2}\tr_K\alpha.
\]
For large $L$ this contradicts the maximum principle. To attain the
maximum on $\Omega_L$, first test with the additional penalty $-\delta f$;
its Hessian term is controlled by $C\delta L^{-2}\tr_K\alpha$, its
gradient terms by the same bound after Young's inequality; then let
$\delta\downarrow0$. At $t=0$, $W=1$ and $Q\ge0$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{flow:eigenvalue-bound}
 K\le e^{J(Q)}\alpha.
\end{equation}
With the bounded choice of $\phi$, $Q$ is bounded above by
\eqref{flow:upper-potentials}. Thus \eqref{flow:eigenvalue-bound} gives
an upper metric bound. The lower bound for $P$, hence for
$\det K/\det\alpha$, then bounds the least eigenvalue below.

\paragraph{Continuation in uniform holomorphic charts.}Let $\iota$ be
the real symmetric representation of a Hermitian matrix and write
\[
 F(B)=\tfrac12\log\det_{\R}B,\quad
 S(x,t)=\iota(\alpha-t\Ric(\alpha)),\quad
 T(D^2U)=\iota(\dd U).
\]
Then $U_t-F(S+T(D^2U))=-\log\det_{\C}\alpha$. Two-sided metric
equivalence puts $S+T(D^2U)$ in a fixed compact convex positive matrix
range. The function $F$ is smooth, concave and uniformly elliptic near
this range. The map $T$ is linear, preserves nonnegativity, and has
norms comparable to the original on nonnegative real symmetric matrices.
The smooth background coefficients have uniform local H\"older bounds.
These are the hypotheses of the transformed-Hessian parabolic estimate
\cite[Theorem~5.1]{ChuRegularity}: its bound for spatial second
derivatives and first time derivative depends on $\|U\|_\infty$ and
these data, not on a prior bound for the full real Hessian. Schauder
estimates then give higher derivatives and continuation on each fixed
approximating background, as in \cite[proof of Theorem~4.1]{LeeTam}.

For initial estimates, extend $U$ by zero to negative times and use
$S(x,t_+)$ with $t_+=\max(t,0)$. Each fixed approximating solution is
smooth one-sided at zero by short-time existence. Since
$U(\cdot,0)=U_t(\cdot,0)=0$, its spatial second derivatives and first
time derivative extend continuously by zero. The extension is $C^2$ in
the parabolic sense required by Theorem~5.1 of \cite{ChuRegularity} and
solves the extended equation also at zero. The time-Lipschitz background
satisfies the H\"older hypothesis, so the same estimate crosses the
initial surface. Spatial differentiation and the linear equation for
$P$ give successive initial Schauder estimates. On each fixed compact
these estimates are uniform in $L$ once the local metric bounds below
hold and $\alpha=g$ there. Thus the approximating flows exist on
$[0,D/2]$ for all sufficiently large $L$ given the chosen $D$.

\subsection{The local limit and its potential estimates}\label{sec:flow:locallimit}

The bounds we need to pass to a limit use other choices of $\phi$. For
any canonical section $S$ from Proposition~\ref{prop:initial-data}, take
\begin{equation}\label{flow:singular-weight}
 \phi=\log|S|_g^2-nf-\epsilon u^2-A_\epsilon,
 \qquad 0<\epsilon\le c_0/D,\quad
 A_\epsilon=C_S'(1+\epsilon^{-1}).
\end{equation}
The growth bound for $S$ makes $\phi\le0$ after choosing $C_S'$. Since
$|\dd u^2|_g\le C$, the canonical-current inequality gives
\[
 \alpha-D\Ric(\alpha)+D\dd\phi
 \ge\alpha-CD\epsilon g\ge\tfrac12\alpha
\]
for small $c_0$. The comparison is made away from the zeros of $S$,
where $\phi$ is smooth; the downward poles localize it away from those
zeros. Finite sums of squared norms may be used as well.

Fix a compact set on which the chosen section does not vanish. For all
large enough $L$ it lies in $\{u<L/2\}$, where $f=0$.
Equation~\eqref{flow:upper-potentials} bounds $Q$ above there by a
constant independent of $L$. Equation~\eqref{flow:Q-comparison} bounds
$P$ below there, and \eqref{flow:eigenvalue-bound} gives a local upper
metric bound with $e^{J(Q)}\to1$. The determinant lower bound then
gives a positive local lower metric bound. Finitely many nonvanishing
sections cover any compact set. Local parabolic estimates on slightly
larger compact sets give all derivatives, including the one-sided
initial estimates, uniformly in $L$. A diagonal subsequence over
compacts and over increasing horizons converges smoothly to $U,P,h$ with
$h_0=g$. The approximations have $\alpha=g$ on each fixed compact for
large $L$, so \eqref{flow:approximation} passes to the K\"ahler
equation there. The limit of \eqref{flow:eigenvalue-bound} is
$h_t\le g$; the limits of \eqref{flow:upper-potentials} give $P\le0$
and $P_t\le0$. This proves \eqref{flow:identities}.

The $Q$ comparison passes to the limit. For a weight
$\phi=\log|S|_g^2-\epsilon u^2-A_\epsilon$ and $t\le D/2$, using $U\le0$,
\begin{equation}\label{flow:rho-pointwise}
 \rho(x,t)\le\frac{-D\phi(x)+nt}{D-t}
 \le2\bigl(-\log|S(x)|_g^2+\epsilon u(x)^2+A_\epsilon+n\bigr).
\end{equation}
Take $D=2(T+1)$, and on a ball of radius $L$ take $\epsilon\asymp L^{-1}$
with $\epsilon\le c_0/D$. Then Proposition~\ref{prop:initial-data}
bounds the average of the right side by $C_TL$, and bounded radii are
absorbed into the constant; this proves \eqref{flow:rho-average}. At
fixed $x$, choose $S(x)\ne0$, put $D=2(t+1)$, $\epsilon=c_0/D$; the same
inequality gives $\rho(x,t)\le C_x(1+t)$.

\paragraph{A positive proper supercaloric function.}
Replace $\log|S|_g^2$ by $\Xi$. For every small enough $\epsilon>0$ the
smooth $\phi_\epsilon=\Xi-\epsilon u^2-A_\epsilon$ is nonpositive after
a finite constant shift and gives a smooth nonnegative $Q_\epsilon$
satisfying the differential inequality in \eqref{flow:Q-comparison} with
$\alpha=g$. The difference
\[
 Q_\epsilon-Q_{\epsilon/2}=\frac{D\epsilon}2u^2
 +D(A_\epsilon-A_{\epsilon/2})
\]
shows that $Q_\epsilon\ge cu^2-C$ because $Q_{\epsilon/2}\ge0$. Adding
a constant gives a positive $Q$ satisfying
\eqref{flow:proper-supercaloric}. This finishes the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:localized-flow}.

\subsection{A cutoff adapted to the evolving metric}\label{sec:flow:cutoff}

The comparison arguments below require a positive integrable weight
$\chi$ with $\dd\chi\le C\chi h_t$. A polynomial $u^{-k}$ gives only a
Hessian bound by $Cu^{-k-2}g$, and $h_t$ need not be uniformly
comparable to $g$ at infinity. We build a function $H$ whose complex
Hessian approaches $g-h_t$ at infinity. The factor
$\exp(-AH/u^2)$, for suitable $A>0$, will then contribute
$-Au^{-2}(g-h_t)$ to the logarithmic Hessian of $\chi$ and absorb errors
measured in the initial metric.

Fix $T<\infty$ and, if necessary, work on a slightly larger finite time
interval. Put
\[
 \eta_t=g-h_t,\qquad a_t=\tr_g\eta_t.
\]
The forms $\eta_t$ are nonnegative, closed, bounded by $g$, with traces
$0\le a_t\le n$. The potential identity gives
\[
 a_t=tR_g-\Delta_gU.
\]
Integrate against a cutoff from Section~\ref{sec:preliminaries} equal to
one on $B(o,L)$, use $-U=\int_0^t\rho(\cdot,s)\,ds$,
\eqref{prelim:averages} and \eqref{flow:rho-average}. Moving the
Laplacian onto the cutoff gives
\begin{equation}\label{flow:eta-average}
 \frac1{\Vol B(o,L)}\int_{B(o,L)}a_t\,dV_g
 \le C_Tt/L\qquad(L\ge1,\ 0\le t\le T).
\end{equation}

For large dyadic $L$, define
\[
 H_{L,t}=-\int_0^{L^2}H_b a_t\,db.
\]
There are $\delta,\beta>0$ with, on $B(o,C_0L)$,
\begin{align}
 |H_{L,t}|+L|\partial H_{L,t}|_g
  &\le C_TL^{2-\delta}t^\beta,
  \label{flow:heat-correction-size}\\
 \dd H_{L,t}&=\eta_t+O(t/L)g,
  \label{flow:heat-correction-hessian}\\
 \partial_tH_{L,t}&\ge H_{L,t}/t-C_TL\qquad(t>0).
  \label{flow:heat-correction-time}
\end{align}

\paragraph{Heat estimates.}
Gaussian upper bounds and relative volume comparison combined with
\eqref{flow:eta-average} give, for some fixed $p>2$,
\begin{equation}\label{flow:heat-integrand}
 H_ba_t(x)\le
 C_T\min\Bigl\{1,\frac tL\Bigl(\frac L{\sqrt b}\Bigr)^p\Bigr\},
 \qquad x\in B(o,C_0L),\quad 0<b\le L^2.
\end{equation}
When $\sqrt b\ll L$, the ball $B(x,\sqrt b)$ may be small relative to
$B(o,L)$; relative volume comparison loses at most a fixed power of
$L/\sqrt b$, and outside $B(o,CL)$ Gaussian annuli absorb both that
power and the polynomial volume growth. The estimate $a_t\le n$ gives
the first term in the min. For the gradient, use the heat-kernel bound
\[
 |\nabla_xH_b(x,y)|
 \le \frac C{\sqrt b}\,
 \frac{\exp(-c\,d_g(x,y)^2/b)}
      {\sqrt{\Vol B(x,\sqrt b)\Vol B(y,\sqrt b)}}.
\]
It follows from \cite[Theorem~1.3, equation~(1.6)]{SoupletZhang} and
the Gaussian upper bound in \cite[Corollary~3.1]{LiYau}, absorbing
$1+d_g(x,y)^2/b$ into the Gaussian. Integrating
its absolute value against $a_t\ge0$ with the same relative-volume and
annular estimates gives $C_Tb^{-1/2}(t/L)(L/\sqrt b)^p$; increase the
fixed $p>2$ if needed. The separate bound $a_t\le n$ gives
$Cb^{-1/2}$. So the gradient obeys the minimum in
\eqref{flow:heat-integrand}, multiplied by $C/\sqrt b$. Splitting both
integrals at $b_*=L^2(t/L)^{2/p}$ shows that their bounds are
respectively
\[
 C_TL^{2-2/p}t^{2/p},\qquad C_TL^{1-1/p}t^{1/p}.
\]
After increasing the finite-time constant we can take $\delta=\beta=1/p$
in \eqref{flow:heat-correction-size}. Lemma~\ref{lem:hodge-heat} gives
\[
 \dd H_{L,t}=\eta_t-\mathcal H_{L^2}\eta_t.
\]
The last form is nonnegative with trace bounded by $C_Tt/L$ by
\eqref{flow:heat-integrand}; this proves
\eqref{flow:heat-correction-hessian}.

\paragraph{The time derivative.}The trace identity also gives
\begin{equation}\label{flow:H-potential-identity}
 H_{L,t}=-t\int_0^{L^2}H_bR_g\,db+H_{L^2}U-U.
\end{equation}
To justify it, insert initial-metric cutoffs in
$\int_0^{L^2}H_b\Delta_gU\,db=H_{L^2}U-U$. Both $U$ and $\Delta_gU=tR_g-a_t$
have polynomially bounded absolute ball integrals, by
\eqref{flow:rho-average}, \eqref{prelim:averages}, and $0\le a_t\le n$.
Gaussian bounds for the scalar heat kernel and its gradient, together
with the absolute Laplacian bounds of the cutoffs, make the shell errors
tend to zero. The identity propagates distributionally and then smoothly
locally. Differentiating it in $t$ uses the same ball-integral bounds on
$P=-\rho$; alternatively integrate over a time interval and then
differentiate locally. Subtracting $H_{L,t}/t$ gives
\[
 \partial_tH_{L,t}-H_{L,t}/t=H_{L^2}(P-U/t)-(P-U/t).
\]
Since $P_t\le0$, $P-U/t\le0$ and $0\le U/t-P\le\rho$. Its heat average
is at most $C_TL$, by \eqref{flow:rho-average} and Gaussian annular
summation. The unaveraged term has the favorable sign. This proves
\eqref{flow:heat-correction-time}.

\paragraph{Gluing.}Choose a nonnegative smooth dyadic partition of unity
$\zeta_L(u)$ with scales $L\asymp u$ and put
$H=\sum_L\zeta_L(u)H_{L,t}$ (finitely many initial scales on any fixed
compact). The partition derivatives have sizes $O(u^{-1})$ in gradient
and $O(u^{-2})$ in complex Hessian.
\eqref{flow:heat-correction-size}--\eqref{flow:heat-correction-time}
give
\begin{equation}\label{flow:glued-correction}
 \begin{gathered}
 |H|\le C_Tu^{2-\delta}t^\beta,\qquad
 |\partial H|_g\le C_Tu^{1-\delta}t^\beta,\\
 \dd H=\eta_t+O(u^{-\delta}t^\beta+t/u)g,\qquad
 H_t\ge H/t-C_Tu.
 \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
These are smooth in space and continuously differentiable in time,
one-sided at $t=0$, which is all the integration below uses. In fact
\eqref{flow:H-potential-identity} gives their first time derivatives
in terms of $H_{L^2}P$ and local smooth functions. The uniform absolute
ball-integral bounds for $P$ make that heat average continuous
including at $t=0$, by local convergence and uniform Gaussian tail
estimates. Spatial regularity follows from the heat equation and
\eqref{flow:heat-correction-hessian}.

\begin{lemma}[Integrable comparison cutoff]\label{flow:cutoff-lemma}
For every sufficiently large fixed integer $k$ there is a positive
$\chi(x,t)$ on $M\times[0,T]$, smooth in space and continuously
differentiable in time one-sided at the initial surface, satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{flow:cutoff-bounds}
 \begin{gathered}
 C_T^{-1}u^{-k}\le\chi\le C_Tu^{-k},\qquad
 |\partial\chi|_g\le C_T\chi/u,\\
 \dd\chi\le C_T\chi h_t,\qquad
 \partial_t\chi\le C_T(1+t^{\beta-1})\chi\quad(t>0).
 \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Take
\[
 \chi=u^{-k}\exp(-AH/u^2).
\]
For fixed $A$, \eqref{flow:glued-correction} gives the two-sided size
and gradient estimates. Expanding
$\chi^{-1}\dd\chi=\dd\log\chi+\partial\log\chi\otimes\bar\partial\log\chi$
gives at infinity
\[
 \chi^{-1}\dd\chi\le u^{-2}\{-A(g-h_t)+C_kg+o(1)g\},
\]
uniformly on the fixed time interval. Terms containing $H/u^2$ or its
gradient are $o(u^{-2})g$ after $A$ is fixed. Choose $A>C_k+1$ and then
take $u$ large enough; the negative multiple of $g$ absorbs the
remaining error and leaves $\chi^{-1}\dd\chi\le Au^{-2}h_t$. On the
remaining compact, smoothness and positivity of $h_t$ give the stated
bound. Finally $\chi_t/\chi=-AH_t/u^2$ and
\eqref{flow:glued-correction} give
\[
 \chi_t/\chi\le A|H|/(tu^2)+C_T/u\le C_T(1+t^{\beta-1}).
\]
The exponent $\beta>0$ makes this time coefficient integrable at zero.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Minimality and nonlinear scalar comparison}\label{sec:flow:scalarcmp}

\begin{proposition}[Scalar comparisons]\label{prop:scalar-comparison}
Fix finite $T>0$. For the flow of Theorem~\ref{thm:localized-flow}:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item $\rho$ is the minimal nonnegative solution with zero initial
      values of
      \begin{equation}\label{flow:rho-source}
      (\partial_t-\Delta_{h_t})\rho=\tr_{h_t}\Ric(g).
      \end{equation}
      There are nonnegative smooth supercaloric functions $\rho_j$ on
      $M\times[0,T]$ with
      \begin{equation}\label{flow:rho-tails}
      0\le\rho_j\le\rho,\qquad \rho_j\ge\rho-C_j,\qquad
      \rho_j\longrightarrow0
      \end{equation}
      locally uniformly on $M\times[0,T]$. Each $\rho_j$ has all spatial
      and one-sided time derivatives smooth at $t=0$, bounded on compact
      spacetime sets.
\item Suppose $v$ is upper semicontinuous, bounded on compact spacetime
      sets, satisfies $0\le v\le\rho$, and in the upper viscosity sense
      on $0<t<T$,
      \begin{equation}\label{flow:nonlinear-comparison}
      \partial_tv\le e^{-v}\Delta_{h_t}v+(1-e^{-v})R_{h_t}.
      \end{equation}
      Then $v=0$ on $M\times[0,T)$.
\end{enumerate}
Neither assertion requires completeness of $h_t$.
\end{proposition}

We first prove \textup{(i)} and the main part of \textup{(ii)}, and
state the smooth majorant as a lemma proved in the next subsection.

\begin{lemma}[Smooth majorant]\label{lem:scalar-majorant}
Let $T>0$ and let $v$ be upper semicontinuous, bounded on compact
spacetime sets, and satisfy $0\le v\le\rho$ on $M\times[0,T]$. Suppose
$v$ satisfies \eqref{flow:nonlinear-comparison} in the upper viscosity
sense on $0<t<T$. There is $w$ smooth on $M\times(0,T)$ and continuous
on $M\times[0,T)$, such that
\[
 v\le w\le\rho,\qquad
 w_t=e^{-w}\Delta_{h_t}w+(1-e^{-w})R_{h_t},\qquad
 w(\cdot,0)=0.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:scalar-comparison}]
The Ricci identity $\Ric(h_t)=\Ric(g)+\dd\rho$, traced with $h_t$,
gives \eqref{flow:rho-source} because $\rho_t=R_{h_t}$ and the source is
nonnegative. Solve the source equation with zero initial and lateral
Dirichlet data on a smooth relatively compact exhaustion: first a weak
energy solution \cite[Chapter~III, Theorem~4.1]{LSU}, then local
parabolic regularity. A source need not vanish at the initial lateral
corner, so full corner compatibility is not asserted. The construction
and its energy estimates pass between a finite set of coordinate charts
and require no completeness of the evolving metric. Domain comparison
makes these solutions increase, and comparison with $\rho$ bounds them
above. Their limit $\rho_{\min}$ is the minimal nonnegative potential.
Local estimates give a smooth solution for $t>0$ and continuous zero
initial values, since it is bounded by $\rho=O_K(t)$ on each compact.
The difference $z=\rho-\rho_{\min}$ satisfies $0\le z\le\rho$,
$z_t=\Delta_{h_t}z$, $z(\cdot,0)=0$.

\emph{Mass argument.} Use $\chi$ from Lemma~\ref{flow:cutoff-lemma} with
$k>2n+3$ and a cutoff $p_N=p(u/N)$, equal to one on $u\le N$ and zero on
$u\ge2N$. Set $I_N(t)=\int_Mz\chi p_N\,dV_{h_t}$. Since
$\partial_tdV_{h_t}=-R_{h_t}dV_{h_t}$ and $R_{h_t}\ge0$, integration by
parts on the compact support of $p_N$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{flow:linear-mass}
 I_N'(t)\le C_T(1+t^{\beta-1})I_N(t)+E_N(t),\qquad
 |E_N(t)|\le C_TN^{2n-1-k}.
\end{equation}
The error carries the derivatives falling on $p_N$; its complex
Hessian, including gradient cross terms with $\chi$, has $g$-norm at
most $C_TN^{-k-2}$ on $N\le u\le2N$. The pointwise cofactor identity and
$0<h_t\le g$ give, for any real $(1,1)$-form $B$,
\begin{equation}\label{flow:cofactor}
 |\tr_{h_t}B|\,dV_{h_t}\le C_n|B|_g\,dV_g.
\end{equation}
Indeed diagonalize $h_t$ against $g$; the coefficient multiplying each
diagonal entry of $B$ is the product of the other $n-1$ eigenvalues,
each at most one. This covers $n=1$ too.
Equation~\eqref{flow:rho-average} bounds the integral of $z$ on the
shell by $C_TN^{2n+1}$, proving the error bound.

The mass $I=\int z\chi\,dV_h$ is finite. Its tails tend to zero
uniformly in $t$ by $z\le\rho$, $dV_h\le dV_g$, and the dyadic shell
estimate. Its initial limit is zero by local convergence and this tail
control. Integrate \eqref{flow:linear-mass} from a positive initial
time, send $N\to\infty$, then send that initial time to zero, to get
$I(t)\le\int_0^tC_T(1+s^{\beta-1})I(s)\,ds$. Gronwall gives $I=0$;
positivity of $\chi$ gives $z=0$, hence $\rho=\rho_{\min}$.

\emph{Tails.} Choose smooth spatial cutoffs $0\le\theta_j\le1$
increasing to one, each compactly supported and eventually equal to one
near each fixed compact. Let $\rho_j$ be the minimal zero-data
potential of $F_j=(1-\theta_j)\tr_{h_t}\Ric(g)$. It is nonnegative and
supercaloric. Linearity on each exhaustion domain and monotone limits
decompose $\rho$ into this exterior-source potential and the minimal
potential of $\theta_j\tr_h\Ric(g)$; the latter is at most
$T\sup_{M\times[0,T]}\theta_j\tr_h\Ric(g)<\infty$ by the scalar maximum
principle on the domains. This gives $\rho_j\ge\rho-C_j$. Monotone
convergence of the nonnegative Dirichlet Green integrals, first in the
domains and then in the source cutoffs, gives $\rho_j\downarrow0$
pointwise.

\emph{Initial regularity.} On each fixed compact neighborhood the
coefficients of $\Delta_{h_t}$ and the source $F_j$ are smooth and
uniformly parabolic through $t=0$. The domination
$0\le\rho_j\le\rho=O_K(t)$ supplies continuous zero initial values.
Extend coefficients and source from a coordinate neighborhood to a
smooth uniformly parabolic Cauchy problem and solve with zero initial
\cite[Chapter~IV, Theorem~5.1]{LSU}. Subtract the local particular
solution $\rho_j$ from a smaller neighborhood; the difference is $O(t)$
there and solves the homogeneous equation for $t>0$. A spatially
localized energy estimate from time $\delta>0$, then $\delta\downarrow0$,
puts the difference in the local weak energy class through zero, and
the squared $L^2$ norm of its initial value on the fixed compact is
$O(\delta^2)$. Extend the difference by zero to negative times and
extend the coefficients smoothly; its zero initial trace removes a
distributional boundary term, so it solves the homogeneous equation
across the initial surface, and interior regularity
\cite[Chapter~III, Theorem~12.1]{LSU} makes it smooth there. Adding
back the particular solution gives the one-sided smooth jets.
Then $\partial_t\rho_j(x,0)=F_j(x,0)$ and higher initial derivatives
are determined recursively from
$(\rho_j)_t=\Delta_{h_t}\rho_j+F_j$, smooth in $x$. Dini's theorem on
compact spacetime sets gives the local uniform convergence in
\eqref{flow:rho-tails}. An auxiliary smooth extension across $t=0$ can
be chosen to match these jets if needed; extension by zero to negative
time is not asserted.

\emph{The nonlinear comparison.} Let $w$ be the minorant from
Lemma~\ref{lem:scalar-majorant}: $v\le w\le\rho$, and $w$ solves
\eqref{flow:nonlinear-comparison} with equality. Multiplying by $e^w$
gives the volume-form identity
\begin{equation}\label{flow:nonlinear-volume}
 \partial_t[(e^w-1)dV_{h_t}]=(\Delta_{h_t}w)dV_{h_t},
\end{equation}
where the cancellation uses $\partial_tdV_h=-R_hdV_h$ and
$e^w(1-e^{-w})R_h=(e^w-1)R_h$.

Integrate \eqref{flow:nonlinear-volume} against $\chi p_N$ and move the
Laplacian onto that compactly supported function. Since $w\le e^w-1$ and
$\Delta_h\chi\le C_T\chi$, the interior term is bounded by a constant
times $M_N(t)=\int_M(e^w-1)\chi p_N\,dV_h$; the time derivative of
$\chi$ is bounded by $C_T(1+t^{\beta-1})\chi$; the exterior error is
$O(N^{2n-1-k})$ by $w\le\rho$ and \eqref{flow:cofactor}. Moreover
\[
 0\le(e^w-1)dV_h\le e^\rho dV_h=dV_g.
\]
The weighted mass is finite with uniformly small tails and zero initial
limit (by dominated convergence on compacts and $\chi\asymp u^{-k}$).
The same limiting Gronwall argument forces it to vanish, so $w=0$; then
$0\le v\le w$ gives \textup{(ii)}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Constructing the smooth majorant}\label{sec:flow:majorant}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:scalar-majorant}]
We build the majorant on relatively compact domains and pass to a limit.

\emph{Barriers.} On a smooth relatively compact $\Omega$ and for time
$<T$, consider equality in \eqref{flow:nonlinear-comparison}. Zero and
$\rho$ are respectively sub- and supersolution; check the latter using
$R_{h_t}-\Delta_{h_t}\rho=\tr_{h_t}\Ric(g)\ge0$. The function
$\rho+2\alpha$ is also a supersolution for every $\alpha>0$.

Take constant initial data $\alpha$. On $\partial\Omega$ choose a smooth
$\zeta:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]$ equal to one near zero and supported in
$[0,1]$, and put
\[
 \varphi(x,t)=\rho(x,t)+\alpha-e^{-\alpha}R_g(x)t\zeta(t/\delta).
\]
For $\delta\sup_{\partial\Omega}R_g\le\alpha/2$, this lies between
$\rho+\alpha/2$ and $\rho+\alpha$. It has $\varphi(\cdot,0)=\alpha$ and
$\varphi_t(\cdot,0)=(1-e^{-\alpha})R_g$, so the value and first
equation compatibility conditions are exact.

\emph{Local existence on the fixed domain.}
Use the compatible linear Dirichlet Schauder theorem
\cite[Chapter~IV, Theorem~5.2]{LSU} and its uniform small-time inverse
\cite[Chapter~IV, Theorem~5.4]{LSU}. These Euclidean-domain statements
give the compact-manifold form by the following chart-summation
argument. Choose a partition of unity $\chi_i$ and cutoffs $\psi_i=1$
near $\supp\chi_i$ in finitely many interior and boundary charts.
Local domains are smoothly capped outside these supports, and $\psi_i$
vanishes near every artificial boundary. If $E_i$ is the local zero-data
inverse, $Sf=\sum_i\psi_iE_i(\chi_if)$ satisfies
$LSf=f+\mathcal Rf$ for the global linear operator $L$. The remainder
consists of first- and zeroth-order commutators
$[L,\psi_i]E_i(\chi_if)$. On the source space $f(\cdot,0)=0$, the
local solutions have zero initial trace. Parabolic interpolation and
the uniform inverse bounds give
$\|\mathcal R\|_{C^{\beta,\beta/2}\to C^{\beta,\beta/2}}\le C\epsilon^\theta$
on a time interval of length $\epsilon$, with $\theta>0$; for
$0<\beta<1$, $\theta=(1-\beta)/2$ works. The remainder preserves the
source space, so $S(I+\mathcal R)^{-1}$ is the required global inverse
on a small interval. All constants may depend on $\Omega$.

\emph{Compatible boundary data.}
Extend the boundary data to a smooth $p$ with $p(x,0)=\alpha$ and
$p_t(x,0)=c(x):=(1-e^{-\alpha})R_g(x)$ throughout $\Omega$. In a smooth
boundary collar with projection $\pi$ and cutoff $\vartheta=1$ near the
boundary, take
\[
 p(x,t)=\alpha+tc(x)+\vartheta(x)\{\varphi(\pi(x),t)-\alpha-tc(\pi(x))\},
\]
and $\alpha+tc(x)$ outside the collar. The bracket and its first time
derivative vanish at zero.

Put $L=\partial_t-e^{-p}\Delta_h$ and seek
$v_{\Omega,\alpha}=p+z$ with zero initial and lateral data for $z$:
\[
 Lz=\mathcal N(z):=(e^{-p-z}-e^{-p})\Delta_h(p+z)
 +(1-e^{-p-z})R_h-Lp.
\]
For every such $z\in C^{2+\beta,1+\beta/2}$,
$\mathcal N(z)(x,0)=0$. The linear inverse applies to compatible
sources. If $z(\cdot,0)=0$, then $\|z\|_\infty\le\epsilon\|z_t\|_\infty$;
spatial interpolation and the time H\"older quotient give
\[
 \|z\|_{C^{\beta,\beta/2}}
 \le C\epsilon^{1-\beta/2}\|z\|_{C^{2+\beta,1+\beta/2}}.
\]
On a fixed ball in the latter space, product and composition estimates
therefore give
\[
 \|\mathcal N(z)-\mathcal N(w)\|_{C^{\beta,\beta/2}}
 \le C\epsilon^{1-\beta/2}\|z-w\|_{C^{2+\beta,1+\beta/2}}.
\]
The coefficient of every highest-derivative difference has this small
factor. $\mathcal N(0)$ is smooth and vanishes at $t=0$, so its
$C^{\beta,\beta/2}$ norm tends to zero with $\epsilon$. The linear
inverse and the contraction theorem give a local Dirichlet solution;
classical comparison with the barriers gives
$0\le v_{\Omega,\alpha}\le\rho+2\alpha$.

\emph{Continuation.} Write $\Delta_h=a^{ij}\partial_i\partial_j+b^j\partial_j$
in fixed real coordinates and put $q=e^{v_{\Omega,\alpha}}$. Directly,
\[
 q_t-\partial_i\Bigl(\frac{a^{ij}}q\partial_jq\Bigr)
 =\frac{b^j-\partial_ia^{ij}}q\partial_jq+(q-1)R_h.
\]
The barriers bound $q$ above and away from zero. The leading matrix is
uniformly elliptic, and the drift and forcing are bounded independently
of $\nabla q$ on the fixed cylinder. Boundary and interior H\"older
estimates apply \cite[Chapter~III, Theorem~10.1]{LSU}; the
exterior-measure condition holds for the smooth compact boundary. Hence
$v_{\Omega,\alpha}$ is H\"older up to the parabolic boundary. Its
original equation now has H\"older coefficients and source, so
\cite[Chapter~IV, Theorem~5.2]{LSU} gives a controlled
$C^{2+\gamma,1+\gamma/2}$ bound on the closed slab, $0<\gamma<1$. In
particular its second spatial derivatives are bounded.

On slabs separated from the initial surface, differentiated boundary
Schauder estimates give all higher bounds. At a hypothetical positive
maximal time the solution has smooth limiting initial data satisfying
the boundary equation jets, and the same local construction restarts
it. This proves existence up to the prescribed finite horizon. No
constants near the remote boundaries need be uniform as $\Omega$
exhausts $M$.

\emph{Viscosity comparison.} This gives $v\le v_{\Omega,\alpha}$. At a
positive interior maximum of $e^{-Ct}(v-v_{\Omega,\alpha})$, the test
has the same spatial Hessian as $v_{\Omega,\alpha}$; the difference of
the right sides is bounded by a constant times
$v-v_{\Omega,\alpha}$ because the derivative in the value variable is
$e^{-v}(R_h-\Delta_hv_{\Omega,\alpha})$, bounded on the compact slab.
Choose $C$ larger than that bound and use a strict increasing time
penalty. The initial and lateral gaps are positive, so no boundary
maximum interferes. This is the usual viscosity argument
\cite{CIL}.

\emph{Limit on $M$.} Exhaust $M$, let $\alpha\downarrow0$, and use local
uniformly parabolic estimates to extract a smooth interior limit $w$:
\[
 v\le w\le\rho,\qquad
 w_t=e^{-w}\Delta_hw+(1-e^{-w})R_h,\qquad w(\cdot,0)=0.
\]
The limit at the initial surface follows from the barriers on each
compact; no uniform estimates near the remote lateral boundaries are
required.
\end{proof}

% ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
% End of Section 3. Next: the cotangent envelope (discs, variation,
% ellipsoids), then charts, dynamics, uniformization, bibliography.

% =============================================================================
\section{The cotangent envelope: compactness and frames}
\label{sec:discs}

We study the flow through boundary averages of its dual squared norm on
holomorphic cotangent discs. Let $\Delta=\{z\in\C:|z|<1\}$ and $dA$
Euclidean area on $\Delta$, and write $\pi:Y=T^{*(1,0)}M\to M$. Put
\[
 N(x,\xi,t)=|\xi|^2_{h_t^{-1}}.
\]
Then $N(\cdot,t)\ge N(\cdot,0)$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:localized-flow}. The
function $N(\cdot,0)$ is plurisubharmonic: the squared norm on the total
space of a Griffiths-nonpositive bundle has this property, and the dual
of $T^{1,0}M$ carries that curvature sign.

Fix $T<\infty$. Let physical time at a disc center be $s$. For $y\in Y$
and $0<s<T$, let $v^{\mathrm{raw}}(y,s)$ be the infimum of
\begin{equation}\label{var:envelope}
 \bigl\langle N(f(\zeta), s e^{w(\zeta)+\overline{w(\zeta)}})\bigr\rangle,
 \qquad (f(0),w(0))=(y,0),
\end{equation}
over holomorphic discs $f:\overline\Delta\to Y$ and $w:\overline\Delta\to\C$,
smooth on the closed unit disc, with $s e^{w+\bar w}<T$ there. Brackets
mean normalized circle average. Let $v$ be the lower semicontinuous
regularization in $(y,s)$. Constant discs, plurisubharmonicity of
$N(\cdot,0)$, and $N(\cdot,t)\ge N(\cdot,0)$ give
\begin{equation}\label{var:sandwich}
 N(y,0)\le v(y,s)\le v^{\mathrm{raw}}(y,s)\le N(y,s).
\end{equation}
Multiplying the fiber component of any competing disc by a nonzero
complex number shows $v$ is homogeneous of degree two in the fiber. The
assertion at the zero section follows from \eqref{var:sandwich}.

Minimizing boundary averages over discs with a prescribed center is the
Poisson-envelope construction of Poletsky and Rosay
\cite{Poletsky1991,Rosay2003}; see also
\cite[Sections~1--2]{LarussonSigurdsson} for the manifold framework. Here
we prove the differential inequality needed for the evolving norm
directly, with the compactness and quantitative deformation estimates
stated below.

\emph{Goal and obstacle.} We prove $v=N$. This gives the submean
inequality for $N(x,\xi,e^{w+\bar w})$, hence its joint
plurisubharmonicity. The competitors in \eqref{var:envelope} have no
common boundary compact and no common area bound. In
Section~\ref{sec:variation} we localize almost-minimizing discs by
boundary walls and a positive area penalty; the compact containing
their full images may then depend on the penalty. With centers in a
fixed compact coordinate region, the frame estimates used in the
differential inequality must depend on boundary and center data,
independently of the area bound and the compact containing the
interiors. That is what this Section establishes, together with the
cotangent evolution identity used in the estimate. All compactness
arguments use the complete initial metric $g$.

\subsection{The symplectic Hessian operator}\label{sec:discs:symplectic}

The canonical holomorphic bivector on $Y$ is
\[
 \mathcal J=\sum_{i=1}^n\frac{\partial}{\partial x_i}\wedge
 \frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_i}.
\]
We use column matrices for Hermitian forms, so a form with matrix $W$
has quadratic value $v^*Wv$. In a frame $e_1,\ldots,e_{2n}$, write
$\mathcal J=\sum_{A<B}J^{AB}e_A\wedge e_B$ and define
\begin{equation}\label{disc:p-definition}
 p(W,\mathcal J)=
 \sum_{A<B,\,C<D}\overline{J^{AB}}J^{CD}(W_{AC}W_{BD}-W_{AD}W_{BC}).
\end{equation}
This is the induced exterior-square squared norm when $W$ is positive,
and a real polynomial for every Hermitian $W$. It is independent of
frame. In canonical cotangent coordinates abbreviate it to $p(W)$. If
$W=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A&B\\B^*&C\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, then
\begin{equation}\label{disc:p-block}
 p(W)=\tr(AC^{\mathsf T})-\sum_{i,j}B_{ij}\overline{B_{ji}}.
\end{equation}
The transpose records the tangent--cotangent pairing. In particular, if
tangent coefficients have metric matrix $h$, the column matrix of the
dual norm is $b=h^{-\mathsf T}$.

\begin{lemma}[Symplectic evolution]\label{lem:symplectic-evolution}
For the flow of Theorem~\ref{thm:localized-flow},
\begin{equation}\label{disc:symplectic-evolution}
 \partial_tN=p(\dd_YN).
\end{equation}
For every real smooth base function $d=d(x,t)$, with $t$ held fixed in
the spatial Hessian,
\begin{equation}\label{disc:base-addition}
 p(\dd_Y(N+d))=\partial_tN+\Delta_{h_t}d.
\end{equation}
These identities do not assume that $h_t$ is complete or has
nonnegative curvature at positive time.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix $(x,t)$ and use K\"ahler-normal coordinates for $h_t$ there. Then
$h_t=b_t=I$, all first spatial derivatives of $b_t$ vanish, and the
Hessian of $N$ has blocks
\[
 \dd_YN=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal R_\xi&0\\0&I\end{pmatrix}.
\]
The horizontal form $\mathcal R_\xi$ is the bisectional curvature form of
$h_t$ with one pair of indices contracted against the metric dual of
$\xi$. Differentiating $b=h^{-\mathsf T}$ twice at a normal point gives
negative second derivatives of $h$, transposed, which are exactly those
curvature components. The K\"ahler symmetries give
\[
 \tr\mathcal R_\xi=\xi^*\Ric(h_t)^{\mathsf T}\xi.
\]
On the other hand $\partial_th=-\Ric(h)$ implies
$\partial_tb=h^{-\mathsf T}\Ric(h)^{\mathsf T}h^{-\mathsf T}$.
Equation~\eqref{disc:p-block} now proves \eqref{disc:symplectic-evolution}.
Adding a base Hessian changes only the horizontal block, and its
contribution to $p$ is $\tr((\dd_xd)b^{\mathsf T})=\tr(h_t^{-1}\dd_xd)$,
proving \eqref{disc:base-addition}. The calculation is tensorial, so the
normal-coordinate verification proves both identities everywhere.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Compact boundary and bounded area}\label{sec:discs:compact}

\begin{proposition}[Compactness of discs]\label{prop:disc-compactness}
For each compact $K\subset M$ and each $A_0<\infty$ there is a compact
$K'\subset M$ such that every holomorphic disc
$f:\overline\Delta\to M$, smooth up to the circle and satisfying
$f(\partial\Delta)\subset K$ and $\int_\Delta f^*\omega_g\le A_0$,
has $f(\overline\Delta)\subset K'$. Every sequence of such discs has a
subsequence converging holomorphically on compact subsets of $\Delta$.

The same statements hold for discs in $Y$ with compact boundary in $Y$
and with $\int_\Delta(\pi\circ f)^*\omega_g\le A_0$. The resulting
compact may depend on $K$ and $A_0$.
\end{proposition}

The area bound first places part of each disc in a fixed compact. An
inverse-Jacobian estimate for the global injective immersion then
propagates this control away from isolated possible singularities of a
limit; properness of the immersion is not assumed. The
extra-integrability lemma removes those punctures, and a subharmonic
compactness lemma carries the initial control.

\begin{lemma}[Extra integrability]\label{lem:extra-integrability}
Let $(E,k)$ be a smooth Hermitian holomorphic vector bundle on
$\Delta^*=\{0<|z|<1\}$ with Griffiths nonnegative curvature. Suppose
$A:E\to\Delta^*\times\C^q$ is holomorphic, pointwise injective, and has
bounded operator norm. If $a$ is a holomorphic section with
$\int_{\Delta^*}|a|_k^2\,dA<\infty$, then there are $r_0,\sigma>0$ such
that
\begin{equation}\label{disc:extra-integrability}
 \int_{0<|z|<r_0}|a|_k^2|z|^{-\sigma}\,dA<\infty.
\end{equation}
The exponent may depend on the section and the bundle data.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choose a fixed smooth $\chi:\R\to[0,1]$ zero on $(-\infty,-2]$ and one
on $[-1,\infty)$, and set
\[
 \chi_m(z)=\chi(m^{-1}\log|z|),\qquad
 \phi_m(z)=\frac2m\log|z|+|z|^{1/m}.
\]
Writing $r=|z|$, one has
\[
 |\dbar\chi_m|\le\frac{C}{mr},\qquad
 (\phi_m)_{z\bar z}=\frac1{4m^2}r^{1/m-2}.
\]
On a complex curve Griffiths and Nakano nonnegativity coincide. Apply the
bundle-valued H\"ormander estimate to $E$-valued $(1,0)$-forms with the
metric $ke^{-\phi_m}$ and source $a\,\dbar\chi_m\wedge dz$; see
\cite{Hormander} and \cite[Theorem~5.1, Remark~4.5]{Demailly}. One may
use any complete auxiliary K\"ahler metric on $\Delta^*$; in dimension
one its conformal factor cancels both from the norm of the unknown top
form times volume and from the curvature-inverse source pairing times
volume. Dropping the nonnegative curvature of $k$ gives
$v_m$ with $\dbar v_m=a\,\dbar\chi_m$ and
\begin{align}
 \int_{\Delta^*}|v_m|_k^2e^{-\phi_m}\,dA
 &\le C\int_{e^{-2m}<r<e^{-m}}
     |a|_k^2r^{-3/m}e^{-r^{1/m}}\,dA,\label{disc:weighted-solve}\\
 \int_{\Delta^*}|v_m|_k^2r^{-2/m}\,dA
 &\le C\int_{e^{-2m}<r<e^{-m}}|a|_k^2\,dA\longrightarrow0.
 \label{disc:correction-small}
\end{align}
For the second estimate use $r^{-3/m}\le e^6$ on the source annulus and
$e^{-1}\le e^{-r^{1/m}}\le1$; constants are independent of $m$. The
estimate concerns smooth metrics on the punctured domain; no curvature
extension at zero is asserted.

The sections $a_m=\chi_ma-v_m$ are holomorphic and converge to $a$ in
unweighted $L^2$. Near zero they equal $-v_m$, so each has a positive
extra-integrability exponent, namely $2/m$. Since $A$ is bounded,
$Aa_m$ and $Aa$ are ordinary $L^2$ holomorphic vector functions on the
punctured disc, and their negative Laurent coefficients vanish by $L^2$
